\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{ab}}{\mathrm{N_{c}}}\mathbb{1}+d_{abc}\,t^{c},\;\;\;d_{abc}=2\,\Tr\left(\{t^{a},t^{b}\}t^{c}\right).

(A.3b)

It is worthwhile noting that Eq. (
A.3a
) implies that the structure constants are totally antisymmetric in all three indices,
f_{abc}=-f_{acb},f_{abc}=-f_{bac}
etc. Moreover, Eq. (
A.3b
) defines, in analogy to (
A.3a
), the totally
symmetric
constants
d_{abc}
, which form a symmetric adjoint representation of
\rm SU(\mathrm{N_{c}})
2
2

2

For
\rm SU(3)
, a system of three quarks (forming a baryon) can be in four representations. These are found using Young Tableaux techniques (see e.g.,
[
100
]
):
3\times 3\times 3=1\oplus 8\oplus\overline{8}\oplus 10
.
8
and
\overline{8}
are respectively the symmetric and antisymmetric representations. Employing the same techniques one can also compute the dimension of a given representation.
. The corresponding casimir operator can be deduced from Eq. (
A.3b
). It reads
[
250
]
:
C_{As}=(\mathrm{N_{c}^{2}}-4)/\mathrm{N_{c}}
.

Adding up Eqs. (
A.3a
) and (
A.3b
) one obtains the following useful relation for the product of two generators,

t^{a}t^{b}=\frac{\imath}{2}f^{abc}t^{c}+\frac{1}{2}d^{abc}t^{c}+\frac{\delta^{ab}}{2}\mathbb{1}.

(A.4)

Notice lastly that due to the fact that
t^{e}.\,t^{e}
is a scalar (casimir) and
t^{e}
are traceless, both
f_{abc}
and
d_{abc}
are traceless (easily seen from the corresponding expressions in Eqs. (
A.3a
) and (
A.3b
)). i.e.,
f_{aac}=d_{aac}=0
, etc. A number of relations that will be useful in later sections, and appendices, are depicted in Fig.
A.1
.

Figure A.1:
Graphical representation of some important colour relations.

Another interesting example
[
250
]
is when one chooses the matrix
\mathbf{M}
to have only one non–zero component;
\mathbf{M}^{i}_{k}=\delta^{i}_{(j)}\,\delta^{(\ell)}_{k}
, where
j
and
\ell
are assumed to be fixed. Substituting into Eq. (
A.1
) yields

\delta^{i}_{j}\,\delta^{\ell}_{k}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{N_{c}}}\,\delta^{i}_{k}\,\delta^{\ell}_{j}+2\,\left(t^{a}\right)^{i}_{k}\,\left(t^{a}\right)^{\ell}_{j}.

(A.5)

The above relation is known as the Fierz identity and is shown pictorially in Fig.
A.2
.

Figure A.2:
Graphical representation of the Fierz identity.

If we rearrange Eq. (
A.5
) so that the colour matrices are written in terms of the delta functions then we can generalise the Fierz identity to the following useful formula
[
251
]

L\,t^{a}\,Q\,t^{a}\,R=\frac{1}{2}\!\Tr\left(Q\right)L\,R-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{N_{c}}}L\,Q\,R,

(A.6)

where
L,Q
and
R
are any colour matrices. In the special case where both
L
and
R
are unit matrices and
Q=t^{b}
then

t^{a}\,t^{b}\,t^{a}=-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{N_{c}}}\,t^{b},

(A.7)

or
Q=t^{b}\,t^{c}
then

t^{a}\,t^{b}\,t^{c}\,t^{a}=\frac{1}{4}\,\delta^{bc}-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{N_{c}}}\,t^{b}\,t^{c}.

(A.8)

Finally, the calculation of the cross section involves, as we shall see later in Sec.
2.2
, products of traces of colour generators, with each colour summed over. It would thus prove useful to provide the following trace identity
[
251
]
:

\Tr\left(t^{a}\,Q\right)\Tr\left(t^{a}\,R\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\Tr\left(Q\,R\right)-\frac{1}{2\mathrm{N_{c}}}\,\Tr\left(Q\right)\Tr\left(R\right).

(A.9)

Appendix B
Perturbative calculations in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation

In this appendix, we present detailed perturbative calculations of differential cross sections for one of the main two QCD processes discussed in this thesis, namely
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation into hadrons. In addition to the classical Feynman diagrammatic approach of computing matrix elements, we consider the effective eikonal method. We start, in Sec.
B.1
, by laying out the general structure of the latter method, encountering in the way some of the main definitions and relations that will be essential to later calculations. We then compute the matrix element squared (and therefore the differential cross section) at Born level, in Subsec.
B.2.2
.
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
corrections stemming from both real emission and virtual loops are addressed in Subsecs.
B.2.3
and
B.2.3
. Lastly we compute the more complicated
\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{s}^{2}\right)
real emission matrix element, taking into account hard emission contributions.

B.1
Eikonal method

The eikonal method
[
89
]
1
1

1

The eikonal method was originally developed in the context of the non–relativistic theory of potential scattering
[
252
,
253
]
(and references in
[
89
]
).
provides a reliable approximation to the full theory at high energies. The resultant all–orders eikonal matrix element may be written in a closed form. This method is based on the propagator approximation

\frac{\imath}{\left(p\pm k\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\simeq\frac{\imath}{\pm 2\,p.k+\imath\epsilon},

(B.1)

where
p
is an external four–momentum, which is assumed to be on–shell (
p^{2}=0
)
2
2

2

The on–shell (or on mass shell) approximation will be used throughout unless stated otherwise. This approximation becomes almost exact when considering scattering at high energies, which are far larger than the masses of the light quarks (i.e., excluding the top quark).
, and
k
may be a partial sum of internal four--momenta
3
3

3

In cases where
k
is the four–momentum of a single parton, which is
soft
relative to its emitter, this approximation may be referred to as the
soft
approximation.
, thereby neglecting the
k^{2}
term relative to
p\,.\,k
(
k^{2}\ll p.k
). The corresponding eikonal Feynman rules are shown in Fig.
B.1
.

Figure B.1:
Eikonal Feynman rules for QCD: gluons are represented by curly lines and fermions by solid lines. For triple vertices, the colour is read clockwise.

Consider the emission of a soft gluon
k=\left(\omega,\mathbf{k}\right)
off a system of
m
(harder) partons, with momenta
p_{i}=\left(E_{i},\mathbf{p}_{i}\right),i=1,\cdots m
. One can write the corresponding emission amplitude as
[
56
,
130
,
179
,
250
]

\left\lvert m+1\right\rangle=g_{s}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{p_{i}\,.\,\epsilon^{\ast}}{p_{i}\,.\,k}\,\mathbf{T}^{a}_{i}\left\lvert m\right\rangle,

(B.2)

where
\epsilon
is the polarisation vector of the emitted gluon and
\left\lvert m\right\rangle
represents the amplitude prior to emission and is a vector in the colour space. The set of ket vectors
\left\lvert\ell\right\rangle
is assumed to form an orthonormal basis,
\big<\ell^{\prime}\big|\ell\big>=\delta\left(\ell^{\prime}-\ell\right)
, for the colour space. The operator
\mathbf{T}^{a}_{i}
(generator of
\rm SU(3)
group) maps the
m
–dimensional space onto the
(m+1)
–dimensional space due to the emission of one extra gluon off
eikonal
leg
i
. The inverse map, i.e.,
(m+1)
–dimensional space onto
m
–dimensional space, which corresponds to the absorption of a single gluon by an eikonal leg
i
is represented by the hermitian conjugate operator
\left(\mathbf{T}^{a}_{i}\right)^{\dagger}
. We write the product of the two mapping operators as

\left(\mathbf{T}^{a}_{i}\right)^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{T}^{a}_{j}\equiv\mathbf{T}_{i}\,.\,\mathbf{T}_{j}.

(B.3)

In other words,
\mathbf{T}_{i}\,.\,\mathbf{T}_{j}
is symmetric under the interchange of
i
and
j
. Notice that it is possible to reduce the product (
B.3
) into casimirs (scalars) when the dimension
m\leq 4
(coloured partons). For
m>4
the product is matrix-valued. Colour conservation implies that
\left\lvert m\right\rangle
transforms as a colour singlet under
\rm SU(3)
rotations. Schematically

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{T}^{a}_{i}\left\lvert m\right\rangle=0.

(B.4)

The above relation will be of great help in simplifying the calculations of the colour factors associated with Feynman amplitudes.

If the
m
–parton amplitude
\left\lvert m\right\rangle
is represented by
\mathcal{M}_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{m}}
, where the subscripts are the colour indices of the incoming or outgoing partons, then
\mathbf{T}^{a}
is represented by (see Fig.
B.1
):

•

+t^{a}
if the radiating parton is an outgoing quark (incoming antiquark).

•

-t^{a}
if the radiating parton is an incoming quark (outgoing antiquark).

•

+(-)\,\imath\mathbf{f}^{a}
if the radiating parton is an outgoing (incoming) gluon.

The operators
t^{a}
and
-\imath\left(\mathbf{f}^{a}\right)_{bc}\equiv-\imath f^{abc}
are, as before, the colour matrices in the fundamental and adjoint representations respectively. Computing the bra state
\left\langle m+1\right\rvert
and multiplying out, one straightforwardly obtains for the braket, or equivalently the matrix element squared,

\left\lvert\mathcal{M}^{2}_{m+1}\right\rvert=\big<m+1\big|m+1\big>=-g_{s}^{2}\sum_{i<j=1}^{m}\frac{2\,\left(p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}\right)}{\left(p_{i}\,.\,k\right)\left(p_{j}\,.\,k\right)}\Tr\left(\left\langle m\right\rvert\mathbf{T}_{i}\,.\,\mathbf{T}_{j}\left\lvert m\right\rangle\right),

(B.5)

where the sum is over distinct
(ij)
pairs and the factor
2
is due to symmetry in
i\leftrightarrow j
. Recall that we are working with on–shell partons. As such, all self–energy Feynman diagrams whereby
i=j
vanish. Moreover, we have used the following sum over polarisations, in the Feynman gauge (
\xi=1
):

\sum_{\lambda=0}^{3}\left(-1\right)^{\delta_{\lambda 0}}\,\epsilon_{\mu}\left(\lambda,\mathbf{k}\right)\,\epsilon_{\nu}^{\ast}\left(\lambda,\mathbf{k}\right)=-g_{\mu\nu}.

(B.6)

We define the dipole
antenna function
,
\omega_{ij}(k)
, which corresponds to a soft gluon
k
stretching between two eikonal (colour–connected) legs
i
and
j
, as

\omega_{ij}(k)=\frac{\omega^{2}\,\left(p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}\right)}{\left(p_{i}\,.\,k\right)\left(p_{j}\,.\,k\right)}=\frac{a_{ij}}{a_{i}\,a_{j}},

(B.7)

with
a_{ij}=1-\cos\theta_{ij}
,
a_{i}=1-\cos\theta_{i}
,
\theta_{ij}
the angle between
(p_{i},p_{j})
pair and
\theta_{i}
the angle between
p_{i}
and
k
.

In the eikonal approximation, the
(m+1)
–dimensional phase space factorises into an
m
–dimensional phase space factor for the radiating ensemble multiplying a one–dimensional phase space factor for the emitted gluon,

\textrm{\,d}\Phi_{m+1}=\textrm{\,d}\Phi_{m}\,\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}\,2\omega}

(B.8)

Absorbing a factor of
4\pi^{2}
into the definition of
\alpha_{s}/\pi
, we have

\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{4\pi\,\omega}=\frac{\omega\textrm{\,d}\omega}{4\pi}\,\textrm{\,d}\cos\theta\textrm{\,d}\phi=\frac{k_{t}\textrm{\,d}k_{t}}{4\pi}\textrm{\,d}\eta\textrm{\,d}\phi,

(B.9)

where
(\theta,\phi)
are the polar and azimuthal angles of the gluon with respect to (w.r.t.) a chosen axis. The (pseudo)rapidity
\eta
and transverse momentum of the gluon
k_{t}
are related to its energy and angle,
\omega
and
\theta
, via

\eta=-\ln\tan\frac{\theta}{2},\;\;\;k_{t}=\omega\,\sin\theta.

(B.10)

B.2
pQCD:
e^{+}e^{-}

\rightarrow
hadrons

The purpose of this section is to provide a detailed derivation of the full differential cross section for the process
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation into final state hadrons up to
\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{s}^{2}\right)
real radiative corrections. That is, we consider the the emission of up to two real gluons off the initial Born process
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\,\overline{q}
. We also consider the one–loop virtual correction in the eikonal limit. The latter is to demonstrate that for the accuracy sought in this thesis it is sufficient to simply take the virtual correction to be minus the real one. Detailed calculations of the total cross–section up to NNLO (two–loop corrections) can be found in, e.g.,
[
254
,
255
]
.

The perturbative expansion of the cross section for the said process, up to the
n^{th}
order, may be cast in the form

\sigma_{n}^{\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow\rm hadrons}=\sigma^{(0)}+\sigma^{(1)}+\sigma^{(2)}+\cdots+\sigma^{(n)}.

(B.11)

Below, we first present a general form for the
n
gluon emission differential cross section,
\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(n)}
, including the form of the
n
–body phase space. Using the resultant expressions, we then compute the Born term,
\sigma^{(0)}
, the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
correction,
\sigma^{(1)}
, including both real and virtual contributions and finally the
\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{s}^{2}\right)
real contribution,
\sigma_{(2)}
. To simplify the calculations, we shall work at a centre–of–mass energy
s<m^{2}_{Z}
, so that contributions from the Z boson propagator can be neglected, and in the centre--of--mass (cm) frame
4
4

4

Recall that the whole problem is Lorentz invariant, and thus one can work at any chosen frame.
, such that the photon propagator’s momentum is
q=\left(\sqrt{s},\mathbf{0}\right)
.

B.2.1
Generalities

The general form of the total cross section is given in Eq. (
2.28
). At order
\alpha_{s}^{n}
in the perturbative expansion, Eq. (
B.11
), it reads

\sigma^{(n)}=\frac{1}{2s}\int\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}\right\rvert^{2}\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{n}.

(B.12)

n
th
order matrix element squared

The invariant matrix element squared (averaged and summed over initial and final state quantum numbers) may be split into a leptonic part,
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow\gamma^{\ast}
, and a hadronic part,
\gamma^{\ast}\rightarrow q\overline{q}g\,\cdots
:

\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}\right\rvert^{2}=\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,L^{\mu\nu}\,H_{\mu\nu}^{(n)}.

(B.13)

The leptonic tensor,
L_{\mu\nu}
, is given by

\displaystyle L^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\,L^{\mu}\left(q_{1},s_{1};q_{2}.s_{2}\right)\,L^{\nu\dagger}\left(q_{1},s_{1};q_{2},s_{2}\right),

(B.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2}}{4}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\left[\overline{u}\left(s_{1},q_{1}\right)\gamma^{\mu}v\left(s_{2},q_{2}\right)\right]\left[\overline{v}\left(s_{2},q_{2}\right)\gamma^{\nu}u\left(s_{1},q_{1}\right)\right],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2}}{4}\,\Tr\left(q_{1}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty\,\gamma^{\mu}\,q_{2}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty\,\gamma^{\nu}\right)=e^{2}\left(q_{1}^{\mu}q_{2}^{\nu}+q_{1}^{\nu}q_{2}^{\mu}-g^{\mu\nu}q_{1}\,.\,q_{2}\right),

where we have used the spinors completeness relations, for a four-momentum
p
,

\sum_{\lambda=\pm 1}u_{\lambda}(p)\overline{u}_{\lambda}(p)=p\!\!\!/\penalty,\;\;\;\sum_{\lambda=\pm 1}v_{\lambda}(p)\overline{v}_{\lambda}(p)=p\!\!\!/\penalty,

(B.15)

along with the recursive relation for the trace of products of an even number,
N
, of Dirac matrices

\Tr\left(\gamma^{\mu_{1}}\gamma^{\mu_{2}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{k}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{N}}\right)=4\,\sum_{k=2}^{n}\left(-1\right)^{k}g^{\mu_{1}\mu_{k}}\,\Tr\left(\gamma^{\mu_{2}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\mu_{k+1}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{N}}\right).

(B.16)

To simplify the calculations, particularly of
H_{\mu\nu}^{(n)}
, it is instructive to employ the Ward identity
[
93
]
, which states that if a matrix element
\mathcal{M}(p)=\mathcal{M}^{\mu}(p)\,\epsilon_{\mu}(p)
then upon the replacement
\epsilon_{\mu}\rightarrow p_{\mu}
the matrix element vanishes. This implies, in our case, that

q_{\mu}\,L^{\mu\nu}\left(e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow\gamma^{\ast}\right)=0,\;\;\;q^{\nu}\,H_{\mu\nu}\left(\gamma^{\ast}\rightarrow q\overline{q}g\cdots\right)=0.

(B.17)

The hadronic tensor is thus proportional to the projector
\left(g^{\mu\nu}-q^{\mu}q^{\nu}/q^{2}\right)
5
5

5

So is the leptonic tensor. However since one will be integrating over final state phase space then one only needs to extract the tensor structure of the final state term. i.e., the hadronic tensor.
. The matrix element squared (
B.13
) can then be written as

\displaystyle\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,L^{\mu\nu}\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\frac{q_{\mu}q_{\nu}}{q^{2}}\right)\widetilde{H}^{(n)},

(B.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,g_{\mu\nu}L^{\mu\nu}\,\widetilde{H}^{(n)},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s^{2}}\,g_{\mu\nu}L^{\mu\nu}\,\frac{1}{3}g^{\rho\sigma}H_{\rho\sigma}^{(n)},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3\,s^{2}}\,L\,H^{(n)},

where we made use of Eq. (
B.17
) to pass to the second line. We then multiplied both sides in the expansion of the hadronic tensor
H_{\mu\nu}^{(n)}
in terms of the aforementioned projector (
H_{\mu\nu}^{(n)}=(g_{\mu\nu}-q_{\mu}q_{\nu}/q^{2})\widetilde{H}^{(n)}
) by the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
and used the fact that
g_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}=4
(in four–dimensional spacetime) to pass to the third line. The last line in Eq. (
B.18
) defines the scalars
L
and
H^{(n)}
:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\nu\nu}\,L_{\mu\nu}=-e^{2}\,s,

\displaystyle H^{(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\,H_{\mu\nu}^{(n)}.

(B.19)

Our task in the subsequent sections is simply to compute
H^{(n)}
.

n–body phase space

The general form of the final state phase space factor for
n
gluons emission is given in Eq. (
2.29
). There are two ways of simplifying the
n
–body form (
2.29
). The first is to proceed by integrating out
\mathbf{p}_{n}
then
\mathbf{p}_{n-1}
and so on. The second method is to exclusively integrate out
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
leaving the rest intact. As we shall see below, this latter method results in an expression analogous to what would be found using the eikonal method of Sec.
B.1
(modulo a Jacobi factor). The first method is thoroughly presented in
[
256
]
. We only explicitly derive the integrated two– and three–body phase space factors for the on–shell case. The two–body phase space reads

\displaystyle\int\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{1}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{1}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{2}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{2}}\,\left(2\pi\right)^{4}\delta^{(4)}\left(q-p_{1}-p_{2}\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{1}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{1}}\,\left(2\pi\right)\,\frac{\delta\left(\sqrt{s}-E_{1}-E_{2}\right)}{2\,E_{2}}\Big|_{\mathbf{p}_{1}=-\mathbf{p}_{2}}=\frac{1}{32\pi^{2}}\int\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{1},

Similarly the three–body phase space is given by

\int\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{3}=\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{1}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{1}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{2}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{2}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{3}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\,E_{3}}\,\left(2\pi\right)^{4}\delta^{(4)}\left(q-p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}\right).

(B.21)

Integrating
\mathbf{p_{3}}
out and using the fact that
6
6

6

This may be shown to hold by noting that
\delta^{(4)}(p^{2})=\delta^{(4)}(p_{0}^{2}-\left\lvert\mathbf{p}\right\rvert^{2})=1/2\left\lvert\mathbf{p}\right\rvert\delta(p_{0}-\left\lvert\mathbf{p}\right\rvert)
for
p_{0}>0
.

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\left(\sqrt{s}-E_{1}-E_{2}-E_{3}\right)}{2E_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\left[\left(q-p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}\right)^{2}\right]=\delta\left(-s-2p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}+2q\,.\,p_{1}+2q\,.\,p_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s}\delta\left[1-x_{1}-x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}\,a_{12}/2\right],

(B.22)

where
a_{ij}=1-\cos\theta_{ij}
and the energy fraction
x_{i}
is defined by

x_{i}=\frac{2q\,.\,p_{i}}{s}=\frac{2\,E_{i}}{\sqrt{s}}.

(B.23)

We have

\int\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{3}=\frac{s}{16(2\pi)^{5}}\int x_{1}\textrm{\,d}x_{1}x_{2}\textrm{\,d}x_{2}\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{1}\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\,\delta\left[1-x_{1}-x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}\,a_{12}/2\right]

(B.24)

Writing
\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{2}=\textrm{\,d}a_{12}\textrm{\,d}\phi_{12}
, averaging over the latter azimuthal angle and simplifying yields

\int\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{3}=\frac{s}{16(2\pi)^{5}}\int\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\textrm{\,d}x_{2}\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{1}.

(B.25)

The
n
–body phase space (
2.29
), in the second method mentioned above, in
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation is given by the general formula
[
221
]

\int\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{n}=\frac{s^{n-2}}{2^{2n+2}\pi^{3}}\int\prod_{i=3}^{n}\,x_{i}\textrm{\,d}x_{i}\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{i}}{2\pi}\,J^{(n)},

(B.26)

where
J^{(n)}
is the Jacobian resulting from integration over
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
,

J^{(n)}=2\int\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p}_{2}\,\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}\,\delta\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-2\right)\delta\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}\right).

(B.27)

Let us consider the cases
n=3
and
n=4
. Integrating out
\mathbf{p}_{2}
and using Eq. (
B.23
), together with the fact that
E_{2}=\left\lvert\mathbf{p}_{2}\right\rvert
, the
x_{i}
–
\delta
function corresponds, for
n=3
and
n=4
respectively, to

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\left(1-x_{3}\right)}{2-x_{3}a_{13}}\qquad(n=3),

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\left(1-x_{3}-x_{4}\right)+x_{3}x_{4}a_{34}}{2-x_{3}a_{13}-x_{4}a_{14}}\qquad(n=4),

(B.28)

where
a_{ij}=1-\cos\theta_{ij}
. Carrying out the remaining straightforward
\delta
integrals one obtains

\displaystyle J^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4\left(1-x_{3}\right)}{(2-x_{3}a_{13})^{2}},

\displaystyle J^{(4)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\left(1-x_{3}-x_{4}\right)+2x_{3}x_{4}a_{34}}{(2-x_{3}a_{13}-x_{4}a_{14})^{2}}.

(B.29)

B.2.2
Born cross-section

Consider the Feynman diagram, depicted in Fig.
B.2
(a)
, representing the Born amplitude.

Figure B.2:
Feynman diagrams for
(a)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\,\overline{q}
, Born amplitude,
(b)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\overline{q}
, virtual corrections, and
(c)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\bar{q}g
, real emission.

The hadronic tensor
H_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}
, given in Eq. (
B.13
) for
n=0
, reads

\displaystyle H_{\mu\nu}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\sum_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}H_{\mu}^{(0)}\left(p_{1},\lambda_{1},i;p_{2},\lambda_{2},j\right)\,H_{\nu}^{(0)\dagger}\left(p_{1},\lambda_{1},i;p_{2},\lambda_{2},j\right),

(B.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{f=1}^{n_{f}}\,e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\delta_{ij}\delta_{ji}\left[\overline{u}\left(\lambda_{1},p_{1}\right)\gamma^{\mu}v\left(\lambda_{2},p_{2}\right)\right]\left[\overline{v}\left(\lambda_{2},p_{2}\right)\gamma^{\nu}u\left(\lambda_{1},p_{1}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{f=1}^{n_{f}}\,4\,e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\left(p_{1\mu}p_{2\nu}+p_{1\nu}p_{2\mu}-g_{\mu\nu}p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)\,\delta_{ii},

(B.31)

where the quark–photon–antiquark vertex is associated with a factor
\imath ee_{q_{f}}\gamma^{\mu}\delta_{ij}
, with
i,j
being the colour indices of the quark and antiquark. We note that
\delta_{ij}\delta^{ji}=\delta^{i}_{i}=\mathrm{N_{c}}=3
for
\rm SU(3)
. The corresponding hadronic scalar
H^{(0)}
, defined in Eq. (
B.19
), is

H^{(0)}=-\mathrm{N_{c}}\,\sum_{f=1}^{n_{f}}\,4\,e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\,2\,s.

(B.32)

Substituting Eqs. (
B.2.1
) and (
B.32
) back into Eq. (
B.13
), which in turn is substituted together with the phase space factor (
) into Eq. (
B.12
) for
n=0
, yields the total
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\overline{q}
cross section:

\sigma^{(0)}=\frac{4\pi\alpha^{2}}{3s}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,\sum_{f=1}^{n_{f}}e_{q_{f}}^{2},

(B.33)

where
\alpha=e^{2}/4\pi
is the QED coupling constant. In the above equation we have integrated out the solid angle
\int\textrm{\,d}^{2}\Omega_{1}=4\pi
.

B.2.3
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
corrections

We now consider the first order correction to the Born process. The radiative corrections at this order consist of two types of diagrams, shown in Fig.
B.2
(b)
and
(c)
: loop and tree level diagrams. The latter corresponds to real emissions of a gluon, leading to a three–body final state, and the former to virtual corrections to the self energies of the quark–antiquark pair as well as to the interaction vertex, keeping the final state identical to the Born case, i.e., a two–body final state. We first compute the contribution of the real emission diagrams.

Real gluon emission

The
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
real integrated cross section is of the form (
B.12
) with
n=1
. The three–body phase space is given in Eq. (
B.25
). The corresponding
\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{s}\right)
matrix element squared satisfies Eq. (
B.13
) with the hadronic scalar
H^{(1)}
given by:

\displaystyle H^{(1)}=g^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left(H_{1\mu}^{(1)\dagger}+H_{2\mu}^{(1)\dagger}\right)\left(H_{1\nu}^{(1)}+H_{2\nu}^{(1)}\right),

(B.34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{11}^{(1)}+H_{22}^{(1)}+H_{12}^{(1)},

where
H_{11},H_{22}
are the “self–energy” diagrams and
H_{12}=H_{1}^{\dagger}H_{2}+H_{2}^{\dagger}H_{1}
contains the “interference” diagrams. Applying Feynman rules (Fig.
2.3
) on Fig.
B.2
(c)
we obtain

\displaystyle H_{1\mu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i^{\prime}j}\,g_{s}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{ii^{\prime}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma^{\rho}\,\frac{p^{+}_{13}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{+}_{13}\right)^{2}}\;\gamma_{\mu}\,v(p_{2})\,\epsilon^{\ast}_{\rho}\right],

\displaystyle H_{2\mu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{ij^{\prime}}\,g_{s}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{jj^{\prime}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p^{+}_{23}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{+}_{23}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,v(p_{2})\,\epsilon_{\sigma}^{\ast}\right],

(B.35)

where
p^{\pm}_{ij}\equiv p_{i}\pm p_{j}
. In order to compute the hermitian conjugate we note that:
\left(\gamma_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}=\gamma_{\mu}
and
\left(\overline{u}\gamma_{\mu}v\right)^{\dagger}=\overline{v}\gamma_{\mu}u
. The first term in the right hand side of (
B.34
) reads

\displaystyle H_{11}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{f}e^{2}\,e_{q_{f}}^{2}\,g_{s}^{2}\Tr\left(\mathbf{t}^{a}_{ij}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{ji}\right)\,\frac{8\,p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}}{p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}},

(B.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{f}e^{2}\,e_{q_{f}}^{2}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{8\left(1-x_{1}\right)}{\left(1-x_{2}\right)}.

Note that the
(-)
sign is due to summation over gluon’s polarisations, Eq. (
B.6
). Similarly, the second and third terms are

H_{22}^{(1)}=-\sum_{f}e^{2}\,e_{q_{f}}^{2}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{8\left(1-x_{2}\right)}{\left(1-x_{1}\right)},

(B.37)

and

H_{12}^{(1)}=-\sum_{f}e^{2}\,e_{q_{f}}^{2}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{16\left(1-x_{3}\right)}{\left(1-x_{1}\right)\left(1-x_{2}\right)}.

(B.38)

Summing up the three terms, i.e., substituting Eqs. (
B.36
), (
B.37
) and (
B.38
) back into (
B.34
), yields

H^{(1)}=-\sum_{f}e^{2}\,e_{q_{f}}^{2}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{8\left(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}\right)}{\left(1-x_{1}\right)\left(1-x_{2}\right)}.

(B.39)

Therefore, upon the substitution of Eqs. (
B.25
) and (
B.39
) into Eq. (
B.12
) (for
n=1
) one finally obtains for the
\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha_{s}\right)
differential cross section

\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\sigma^{(1)}}{\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\textrm{\,d}x_{2}}=\sigma^{(0)}\,\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}{\left(1-x_{1}\right)\left(1-x_{2}\right)}.

(B.40)

Examining the above equation one immediately observes that the differential cross section
\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(1)}
diverges whenever
x_{1}
or
x_{2}
approaches one. From Eq. (
B.23
), we have

\frac{s}{2}\left(1-x_{k}\right)=E_{i}\,E_{j}\left(1-\cos\theta_{ij}\right).

(B.41)

The limit
x_{k}\rightarrow 1
corresponds to partons
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
being soft (
E_{i}\,\mathrm{or}\,E_{j}\ll 1
) and/or collinear (
\theta_{ij}\ll 1
). The former limit is termed soft (or infrared) divergence while the latter is referred to as the collinear (or mass) divergence. We show in Fig.
B.3
a “Dalitz” plot of the various soft and collinear limits for the three parton final state.

Figure B.3:
Dalitz plot for a three–body “decay” of a photon. All partons are assumed on–shell. The edges
x_{i}=1
correspond to two partons being collinear and the corners
x_{i}=0
correspond to one parton being soft (
p_{i}\rightarrow 0
). The different kinematical regions are:
(1)\,x_{1}>x_{2}>x_{3},(2)\,x_{2}>x_{1}>x_{3},(3)\,x_{2}>x_{3}>x_{1},(4)\,x_{3}>x_{2}>x_{1},(5)\,x_{3}>x_{1}>x_{2}
and
(6)\,x_{1}>x_{3}>x_{2}
.

These soft and collinear divergences are typical in QCD amplitude calculations (and QED as well). Since experimentally the answer, i.e., the value of the total cross section, is finite one ought to “regularise” the infinities that arise in the theoretical calculations. The final analytical answer should be independent of the “regularisation procedure”, though. An example of a regularisation scheme would be the massive gluon scheme
[
257
,
258
]
, whereby the gluon is given a fictitious mass
m_{g}
. Such a term would simultaneously prevent both infrared and mass singularities. Another much powerful scheme is the dimensional regularisation scheme
[
259
]
, whereby one works in dimension
d=4-\varepsilon
and only at the end one takes the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. The reader is referred to
[
260
]
for more details.

It is possible to construct experimental observables that are insensitive to both infrared and collinear divergences. The distribution of such infrared and collinear (IRC) safe observables, which will be discussed in Chapter
3
,
\textrm{\,d}\sigma/\textrm{\,d}v
produces, upon phase space integration, logarithmic enhanced terms of the form
\alpha_{s}^{n}\ln^{m}\left(v\right)
, where
v
is a member of the class of IRC safe observables. Such terms break the convergence of the perturbative expansion, in terms of the coupling
\alpha_{s}
, and call for a “resummation”.

As a final note, let us consider what would Eq. (
B.40
) reduce to in the eikonal approximation. The latter approximation corresponds to neglecting the terms
p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}
and
p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}
relative to
p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}
in the formulae of
H_{11}^{(1)},H_{22}^{(1)}
and
H_{12}^{(1)}
. In other words,
H^{(1)}\simeq H_{12}^{(1)}
. Consequently the differential cross section (
B.40
) becomes

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1),\mathrm{eik}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{(0)}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\int\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\textrm{\,d}x_{2}\,\frac{2\left(1-x_{3}\right)}{\left(1-x_{1}\right)\left(1-x_{2}\right)},

(B.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s}{2}\int\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\ x_{2}\,\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(0)}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\frac{2\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{p_{3}}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}\,2\,E_{3}}\,\frac{2\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}.

The last equality can straightforwardly be verified using the eikonal techniques of Sec.
B.1
.

\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
virtual gluon corrections

The
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
virtual corrections are depicted in Fig.
B.2
(b)
and correspond to a two–body final state. The associated differential cross section is again of the form

\textrm{\,d}\sigma_{\rm{vir}}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2s}\,\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\rm{vir}}^{(1)}}\right\rvert^{2}\,\textrm{\,d}\Pi_{2},

(B.43)

where the two–body phase space factor has been computed in Eq. (
) and the matrix element squared is given by an expression analogous to (
B.18
), with the real hadronic tensor replaced by the virtual one

H_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}=2\,g^{\mu\nu}\,\mathfrak{Re}\left[H_{\mu}^{(0)\dagger}\left(H_{1\mathrm{vir},\nu}^{(1)}+H_{2\mathrm{vir},\nu}^{(1)}+H_{3\mathrm{vir},\nu}^{(1)}\right)\right].

(B.44)

The pre-factor
2
comes from the fact that the full
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
Feynman amplitude is
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}^{(0)}+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)}+\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}
, which when squared is proportional to
2\,\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathcal{M}^{(0)\dagger}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}\right]\equiv\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}}\right\rvert
. The amplitude
H_{\mu}^{(0)}
is given in Eq. (
B.31
) and (see Fig.
B.2
(b)
)

\displaystyle H_{1\mathrm{vir},\mu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\delta_{ij}\,\overline{u}(p_{1})\left[\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma_{\rho}\frac{p_{1}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty-\ell\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p_{1}-\ell\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma^{\rho}\right]\frac{p_{1}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{p_{1}^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,v(p_{2}),

\displaystyle H_{2\mathrm{vir},\mu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\delta_{ij}\,\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p_{2}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{p_{2}^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\left[\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma_{\rho}\,\frac{p_{2}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty-\ell\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p_{2}-\ell\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma^{\rho}\right]v(p_{2}),

\displaystyle H_{3\mathrm{vir},\mu}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\delta_{ij}\,\overline{u}(p_{1})\left[\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma_{\rho}\,\frac{p_{1}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty+\ell\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p_{1}+\ell\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p_{2}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty-\ell\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p_{2}-\ell\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\gamma^{\rho}\right]

\displaystyle\times v(p_{2}).

Substituting Eq. (
B.31
) and the terms in Eq. (
) into Eq. (
B.44
) and simplifying the traces one finds, for the scalars
H_{i,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}=g^{\mu\nu}\,H_{\mu}^{(0)\dagger}\,H_{i\mathrm{vir},\nu}^{(1)}
,

\displaystyle H_{1,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\frac{-16\,s}{p_{1}^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\frac{p_{1}\,.\,\ell}{\left(p_{1}-\ell\right)^{2}+\imath\epsilon},

\displaystyle H_{2,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{1,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}\big|_{1\rightarrow 2},

\displaystyle H_{3,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\frac{-32\left[p_{1}\,.\,\left(p_{2}-\ell\right)\right]\left[p_{2}\,.\,\left(p_{1}+\ell\right)\right]}{\left[(p_{1}+\ell)^{2}+\imath\epsilon\right]\left[(p_{2}-\ell)^{2}+\imath\epsilon\right]}.

The Block–Nordsieck theorem
[
261
]
states that the logarithmically enhanced (divergent) contributions due to real emission of collinear and soft gluons cancel against the corresponding virtual corrections. This can be explicitly checked for our example,
e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\overline{q}g
, using one of the aforementioned regularisation schemes along with Feynman parametrisation techniques. We refer the reader to
[
260
]
for full details. Since the singular logarithmic contributions can be fully captured in the eikonal approximation, it is sufficient to verify the cancellation using the latter method. To this end, the self–energy terms in Eq. (
) vanish while the vertex term reduces to

H_{3,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}=e^{2}e^{2}_{q_{f}}\,\mathrm{N_{c}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{4}\ell}{\left(2\pi\right)^{4}}\,\frac{-\imath}{\ell^{2}+\imath\epsilon}\,\frac{2\,s^{2}}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,\ell+\imath\epsilon\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,\ell-\imath\epsilon\right)},

(B.47)

where we have used
p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}=s/2
(from momentum conservation
\delta^{(4)}\left(q-p_{1}-p_{2}\right)
). We can simplify the integral in Eq. (
B.47
) using contour integration. We first specialise to the cm frame,
\mathbf{p_{1}}+\mathbf{p_{2}}=\mathbf{0}
, and write

H_{3,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}\propto\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{\ell}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}}\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}\ell_{0}}{2\pi}\,\frac{-\imath}{\left(\ell_{0}-\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert-\imath\epsilon\right)\left(\ell_{0}+\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert+\imath\epsilon\right)}\frac{8\,s}{\left(\ell_{0}-\ell_{3}+\imath\epsilon\right)\left(\ell_{0}+\ell_{3}-\imath\epsilon\right)}.

(B.48)

The above integral has four simple poles in the
\ell_{0}
complex plane (Fig.
B.4
).

Figure B.4:
Poles of the integrand of Eq. (
B.48
) in the complex plane.

Closing the contour in the upper half, thus picking up only two poles, and applying the residue theorem
7
7

7

For more details on the residue theorem and complex integration techniques, see for instance
[
262
]
.
one finds

H_{3,\mathrm{vir}}^{(1)}\propto 8\,s\,\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{\ell}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}}\,\frac{1}{2\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell_{\perp}}\right\rvert^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert}-\frac{1}{\ell_{3}-\imath\epsilon}\right),

(B.49)

where the invariant transverse momentum
\ell_{\perp}
with respect to an
(ij)
dipole cm frame is related to the antenna function, Eq. (
B.7
), through

\frac{2}{\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell_{\perp}}\right\rvert^{2}}=\frac{\left(p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}\right)}{\left(p_{i}\,.\,\ell\right)\left(p_{j}\,.\,\ell\right)}=\frac{\omega_{ij}(\ell)}{E^{2}_{\ell}},

(B.50)

with
E_{\ell}
being the energy of the gluon
\ell
. Substituting Eq. (
B.49
) into Eq. (
B.44
), then plug into Eq. (
B.43
) results in the following expression for the (integrated) eikonal virtual cross section

\sigma_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1),\mathrm{eik}}=-\sigma^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\,\mathfrak{Re}\left\{\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{\ell}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}2\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert}\frac{2\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,\ell\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,\ell\right)}\left[1-\frac{\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert}{\ell_{3}-\imath\epsilon}\right]\right\}.

(B.51)

The term involving the
z
–component of the gluon momentum,
\ell_{3}
, in the square bracket of Eq. (
B.51
) can be simplified further. The important point is to explicitly keep the
\imath\epsilon
prescription. That is, from Eq. (
B.49
) one has

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{\ell}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\left\lvert\ell_{\perp}\right\rvert^{2}}\,\frac{1}{\ell_{3}-\imath\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\ell_{\perp}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\left\lvert\ell_{\perp}\right\rvert^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\textrm{\,d}\ell_{3}\,\frac{1}{\ell_{3}-\imath\epsilon},

(B.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\ell_{\perp}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\left\lvert\ell_{\perp}\right\rvert^{2}}\,\lim_{a\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{-a}^{a}\textrm{\,d}\ell_{3}\,\frac{\ell_{3}+\imath\epsilon}{\ell_{3}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\ell_{\perp}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{3}2\left\lvert\ell_{\perp}\right\rvert^{2}}\,\imath\pi.

Thus the latter term is purely imaginary. It is worthwhile noting that the
\imath\pi
factor is related to super–leading logarithms and the possibility of factorisation breaking
[
127
,
128
,
243
,
244
,
263
]
. The final form of the virtual cross section reads

\sigma_{\mathrm{vir}}^{(1),\mathrm{eik}}=-\sigma^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\int\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\mathbf{\ell}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}2\left\lvert\mathbf{\ell}\right\rvert}\frac{2\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,\ell\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,\ell\right)}=-\sigma_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1),\mathrm{eik}}.

(B.53)

B.2.4
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
corrections

There are two processes contributing to the amplitude at this order, as depicted in Fig.
B.5
:
(a)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q(p_{1})\overline{q}(p_{2})g(p_{3})g(p_{4})
and
(b)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q(p_{1})\bar{q}(p_{2})q^{\prime}(p_{3})\overline{q}^{\prime}(p_{4})
. The integrated cross section assumes the general form (
B.12
) (
n=2
) with the four–body phase space factor given in Eqs. (
B.26
) and (
B.29
). We begin by addressing the process
(a)
.

Figure B.5:
Feynman diagrams for
(a)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\overline{q}gg
and
(b)\,e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow q\bar{q}q^{\prime}\overline{q}^{\prime}
. There are three other diagrams corresponding to swapping the secondary gluons
g_{3},g_{4}
in the top three diagrams.

The hadronic scalar
H^{(2)}
is

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{8}H_{a,i\mu}^{(2)}\right]^{\dagger}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{8}H_{a,j\nu}^{(2)}\right],

(B.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{8}H_{a,ij}^{(2)},

where, starting from top left in Fig.
B.5
,

\displaystyle H_{a,1\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\frac{p^{+}_{14}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p_{14}^{+}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\beta}\,\frac{p^{++}_{134}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{134}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,v(p_{2})\right]\epsilon^{\ast\alpha}_{4}\epsilon^{\ast\beta}_{3},

\displaystyle H_{a,2\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\frac{p^{+}_{14}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{+}_{14}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p^{+}_{23}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{+}_{23}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\beta}\,v(p_{2})\right]\epsilon_{4}^{\ast\alpha}\epsilon_{3}^{\ast\beta},

\displaystyle H_{a,3\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p^{++}_{234}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{234}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\frac{p^{+}_{23}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{+}_{23}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\beta}\,v(p_{2})\right]\epsilon^{\ast\alpha}_{4}\epsilon^{\ast\beta}_{3},

\displaystyle H_{a,4\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\imath f^{a_{4}a_{3}a}\mathbf{t}^{a}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\frac{p^{++}_{134}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{134}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,v(p_{2})\right]\epsilon^{\ast\sigma}_{4}\epsilon^{\ast\rho}_{3}\times

\displaystyle\times\;\;\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{\left(p^{+}_{34}\right)^{2}}\left[g_{\beta\sigma}\left(p^{+}_{34}+p_{4}\right)_{\rho}+g_{\sigma\rho}\left(p_{3}-p_{4}\right)_{\beta}-g_{\rho\beta}\left(p^{+}_{34}+p_{3}\right)_{\sigma}\right],

\displaystyle H_{a,5\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\imath f^{a_{4}a_{3}a}\mathbf{t}^{a}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p^{++}_{234}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{234}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,v(p_{2})\right]\epsilon^{\ast\sigma}_{4}\epsilon^{\ast\rho}_{3}\times

\displaystyle\times\;\;\frac{g^{\alpha\beta}}{\left(p^{+}_{34}\right)^{2}}\left[g_{\beta\sigma}\left(p^{+}_{34}+p_{4}\right)_{\rho}+g_{\sigma\rho}\left(p_{3}-p_{4}\right)_{\beta}-g_{\rho\beta}\left(p^{+}_{34}+p_{3}\right)_{\sigma}\right],

\displaystyle H_{a,6\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{a,1\mu}^{(2)}\left(3\leftrightarrow 4\right),

\displaystyle H_{a,7\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{a,2\mu}^{(2)}\left(3\leftrightarrow 4\right),

\displaystyle H_{a,8\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{a,3\mu}^{(2)}\left(3\leftrightarrow 4\right),

(B.55)

where, in analogy to
p_{ij}^{\pm}
, we have defined
p_{ijk}^{\pm\pm}=p_{i}\pm p_{j}\pm p_{k}
and used Feynman gauge (
\xi=1
in Fig.
2.3
) for the gluon propagator. Let us further define
p^{+}_{34}\equiv p
. In the
soft
limit
8
8

8

Unless stated otherwise, this limit is sufficient for the accuracy of most of the calculations performed in this thesis.
:
p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\gg p_{1(2)}\,.\,p_{i}
for
i=3,4
(or equivalently
p\!\!\!/\penalty_{1}^{2},p\!\!\!/\penalty_{2}^{2}\gg p\!\!\!/\penalty_{3}^{2},p\!\!\!/\penalty_{4}^{2}
), Eq. (
B.55
) can be greatly simplified. Employing the Dirac algebra relation, for massless spinors,

\overline{u}(q)\,\gamma^{\alpha}\,q\!\!\!/\penalty=\overline{u}(q)\,2\,q^{\alpha},

(B.56)

and grouping together amplitudes with the same colour factor we write Eq. (
B.55
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,1\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{\mu}^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\left[\frac{p_{1}^{\alpha}}{p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}}\frac{p_{1}^{\beta}}{p_{1}\,.\,p}-\frac{p_{1}^{\alpha}}{p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}}\frac{p_{2}^{\beta}}{p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}}+\frac{p_{2}^{\alpha}}{p_{2}\,.\,p}\frac{p_{2}^{\beta}}{p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}}\right]\epsilon^{\ast}_{3,\beta}\epsilon^{\ast}_{4,\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,2\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{\mu}^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\left[\frac{p_{1}^{\beta}}{p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}}\frac{p_{1}^{\alpha}}{p_{1}\,.\,p}-\frac{p_{1}^{\beta}}{p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}}\frac{p_{2}^{\alpha}}{p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}}+\frac{p_{2}^{\beta}}{p_{2}\,.\,p}\frac{p_{2}^{\alpha}}{p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}}\right]\epsilon^{\ast}_{3,\beta}\epsilon^{\ast}_{4,\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,3\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H_{\mu}^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\imath f^{a_{4}a_{3}a}\mathbf{t}^{a}\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\lambda}}{p_{1}\,.\,p}-\frac{p_{2}^{\lambda}}{p_{2}\,.\,p}\right)\,\epsilon^{\ast\beta}_{3}\epsilon^{\ast\alpha}_{4}\times

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{p^{2}}\Big[g_{\lambda\alpha}\left(p+p_{4}\right)_{\beta}+g_{\alpha\beta}\left(p_{3}-p_{4}\right)_{\lambda}-g_{\beta\lambda}\left(p+p_{3}\right)_{\alpha}\Big],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{\mu}^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\imath f^{a_{3}a_{4}a}\mathbf{t}^{a}\,\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)-\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}{\left(p_{3}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)}\,g_{\alpha\beta}+\cdots\right]\epsilon^{\ast\beta}_{3}\epsilon^{\ast\alpha}_{4},

where the
\cdots
represent the following two terms

\frac{1}{p_{1}\,.\,p}\left[p_{1\alpha}\left(p+p_{4}\right)_{\beta}-p_{1\beta}\left(p+p_{3}\right)_{\alpha}\right]-\frac{1}{p_{2}\,.\,p}\left[p_{2\alpha}\left(p+p_{4}\right)_{\beta}-p_{2\beta}\left(p+p_{3}\right)_{\alpha}\right].

(B.58)

The above terms are linear in four–momenta,
p_{1\alpha(\beta)},p_{2\alpha(\beta)}
, and will hence be ignored, as their contributions will be subleading.

We are now in a position to compute the
soft
hadronic scalar
H_{a}^{(2)}
given in Eq. (
B.54
). Before proceeding we note the following colour products (see Sec.
2.1
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{a_{3}}\mathbf{t}^{a_{4}}\,\imath f^{a_{3}a_{4}a}\mathbf{t}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}}.

(B.59)

We thus write
H_{a}^{(2)}
as a sum of
abelian
and
non–abelian
contributions:

\frac{H_{a}^{(2)}}{g_{s}^{4}}=\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}}\left[\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,11}^{(2)}+2\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,12}^{(2)}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,22}^{(2)}\right]+\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}}\left[\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,33}^{(2)}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,23}^{(2)}-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,13}^{(2)}-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,12}^{(2)}\right].

(B.60)

where the colour–stripped hadronic scalars
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,ij}^{(2)}
are:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,11}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\times\frac{2\,\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,22}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,11}^{(2)}\left(3\leftrightarrow 4\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,12}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\Bigg[\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,11}^{(2)}\,\frac{\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}{2\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)}+\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,11}^{(2)}\,\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)}{2\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}+

\displaystyle\hskip 122.34692pt+\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)}\Bigg],

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,33}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\times 4\,\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)-\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}{\left(p_{3}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)}\right]^{2}+\cdots,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,13}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)-\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}{\left(p_{3}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)}\right],

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{a,23}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)-\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}{\left(p_{3}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)}\right].

Note that a
(-)
sign has been absorbed into the Born hadronic tensor
H^{(0)}
. Substituting Eqs. (
B.2.4
) and (
) into Eq. (
B.60
), which is in turn substituted back into Eq. (
B.13
), for
n=2
and process
a
, yields

\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}_{a}^{(2)}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}\right\rvert^{2}\,g_{s}^{4}\left[4\,\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}}W_{\mathrm{P}}+\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}}\left(2\,S+H_{g}\right)\right],

(B.63)

where the abelian (primary emission) amplitude is

W_{\mathrm{P}}=\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{3}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{3}\right)}\,\frac{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{2}\right)}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p_{4}\right)\left(p_{2}\,.\,p_{4}\right)}=\frac{\omega_{12}(p_{3})\,\omega_{12}(p_{4})}{E_{3}^{2}E_{4}^{2}},

(B.64)

and writing
\left(p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}\right)=\left(ij\right)
the non--abelian (secondary emission) amplitude is
9
9

9

The trick to simplify the non–abelian part of Eq. (
B.60
) to the form presented in Eq. (
B.63
) is to add and subtract a factor of
2
in the square brackets of Eqs. (
B.2.4
) and (
).
the sum of the soft,
S
, and gluon hard,
H_{g}
, terms:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(12)}{(13)(34)(42)}+\frac{(12)}{(14)(43)(32)}-\frac{(12)}{(13)(32)}\frac{(12)}{(14)(42)},

(B.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{E_{3}^{2}E_{4}^{2}}\,\omega_{12}(p_{3})\left[\omega_{13}(p_{4})+\omega_{23}(p_{4})-\omega_{12}(p_{4})\right],

and

H_{g}=2\,\mathcal{I}^{2}-S\,\mathcal{J}-4\frac{\omega_{12}(p)}{(34)E_{p}^{2}},

(B.66)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(13)(24)-(14)(23)}{(34)(1p)(2p)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(13)(24)+(14)(23)}{(1p)(2p)}.

(B.67)

The other process at
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
, as stated above, is represented by the Feynman diagrams in Fig.
B.5
(b)
. The corresponding hadronic scalar reads

\displaystyle H_{b}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left[H_{b,1\mu}^{(2)}+H_{b,2\mu}^{(2)}\right]^{\dagger}\left[H_{b,1\nu}^{(2)}+H_{b,2\nu}^{(2)}\right],

(B.68)

where

\displaystyle H_{b,1\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i^{\prime}_{1}i_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{i_{1}i^{\prime}_{1}}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{j_{3}j_{4}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,\frac{p^{++}_{134}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{134}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,v(p_{2})\right]\frac{1}{\left(p^{+}_{34}\right)^{2}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{4})\,\gamma^{\alpha}\,v(p_{3})\right],

\displaystyle H_{b,2\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\imath ee_{q_{f}}\delta_{i_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}}\,g_{s}^{2}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{i^{\prime}_{2}i_{2}}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{j_{3}j_{4}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\frac{p^{++}_{234}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty}{\left(p^{++}_{234}\right)^{2}}\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,v(p_{2})\right]\frac{1}{\left(p^{+}_{34}\right)^{2}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{4})\,\gamma^{\alpha}\,v(p_{3})\right].

Similar to process
(a)
, Eq. (
) simplifies significantly in the
soft
limit. Adding up the two contributions (and using the above notation
p=p^{+}_{34}
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{b,\mu}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{\mu}^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{2}\,\mathbf{t}^{a}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\mathbf{t}^{a}_{j_{3}j_{4}}\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\alpha}}{\left(p_{1}\,.\,p\right)}-\frac{p_{2}^{\alpha}}{\left(p_{2}\,.\,p\right)}\right)\frac{1}{p^{2}}\left[\overline{u}(p_{4})\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,v(p_{3})\right].

(B.70)

Squaring the above amplitude we find

\displaystyle H_{b}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\,\mathbf{t}^{a}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\Tr\left(\mathbf{t}^{a}_{j_{3}j_{4}}\mathbf{t}^{b}_{j_{3}j_{4}}\right)\mathbf{t}^{b}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\,n_{f}\,\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\alpha}}{(1p)}-\frac{p_{2}^{\alpha}}{(2p)}\right)\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\beta}}{(1p)}-\frac{p_{2}^{\beta}}{(2p)}\right)\times

\displaystyle\frac{\Tr\left[p_{4}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty\,\gamma_{\alpha}\,p_{3}\!\!\!\!\!/\penalty\,\gamma_{\beta}\right]}{4\left(p_{3}\,.\,p_{4}\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle H^{(0)}\,g_{s}^{4}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{T_{R}}n_{f}\,\frac{2}{\left[(34)(1p)(2p)\right]^{2}}\left[(12)(34)(1p)(2p)-\left((13)(24)-(14)(23)\right)^{2}\right].

Therefore the matrix element squared for process
(b)
can be written, following the steps outlined previously for process
(a)
, as

\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}_{b}^{(2)}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}\right\rvert^{2}\,g_{s}^{4}\,\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{T_{R}}n_{f}\,H_{q},

(B.72)

where
\mathrm{T_{R}}=\mathrm{T_{F}}=1/2
and the quark hard term is

H_{q}=-4\,\mathcal{I}^{2}+4\,\frac{\omega_{12}(p)}{(34)E_{p}^{2}}.

(B.73)

Adding up the contributions of both processes
(a)
and
(b)
the final expression of the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
matrix element in the soft limit (which nonetheless includes the quark and gluon hard terms) is given by

\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(2)}}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert\overline{\mathcal{M}^{(0)}}\right\rvert^{2}\,g_{s}^{4}\left[4\,\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}}\,W_{\mathrm{P}}+\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}}\left(2S+H_{g}\right)+\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{T_{F}}n_{f}\,H_{q}\right].

(B.74)

Notice that if one employs the eikonal method (described in Sec.
B.1
) then one would find an identical formula to Eq. (
B.74
) without the hard terms
H_{g}
and
H_{q}
.

Appendix C
Jet–thrust calculations

C.1
Derivation of the LO distribution

In the present section we outline the necessary steps for the derivation of the full logarithmic part of the LO
\tau_{E_{0}}
integrated distribution, Eq. (
5.7
). For the emission of a single gluon, i.e,
e^{+}e^{-}\to q\,\bar{q}\,g
, we define the kinematic variables,
x_{i}=2p_{i}\,.\,q/Q^{2}=2E_{i}/Q
and
y_{ij}=2p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}/Q^{2}=1-x_{k}
where
i,j,k=1(q),2(\bar{q}),3(g)
and
q=p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}
. The leading–order (
q\bar{q}g
) matrix–element squared is given in Eq. (
B.40
). It reads

\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{2}\sigma^{(1)}}{\sigma^{(0)}\textrm{\,d}x_{1}\textrm{\,d}x_{2}}=\frac{\mathrm{C_{F}}\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\frac{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}{(1-x_{1})(1-x_{2})},

(C.1)

where
\sigma^{(0)}
is the Born cross–section. The integration region, which is originally
1\geq x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\geq 0
(recall that
x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=2
) and which leads to divergences, gets modified by introducing the jet shape variable. For three partons in the final state,
\tau_{E_{0}}
is zero unless two partons are clustered together. That is, the
\tau_{E_{0}}
shape distribution receives, at this order, contributions from two–jet configurations only. Three–jet final state configurations, which are also present at this order, do not contribute to the latter distribution. They contribute to the
E_{0}
distribution instead, as we shall see later.

It is a straightforward exercise to show that the thrust, or rather
\tau=1-T
, is, for small values of
\tau
, the sum of the masses of two
hemisphere
jets. Precisely
\tau\simeq(p_{\mathrm{J}_{1}}^{2}+p_{\mathrm{J}_{2}}^{2})/Q^{2}=1-\max\{x_{i}\}=\tau_{E_{0}}
where
i=1,2,3

1
1

1

From conservation of momentum given above we have for a jet formed by a pair of partons
(i,j)
:
\tau_{E_{0}}=(p_{i}+p_{j})^{2}/Q^{2}=2p_{i}\,.\,p_{j}/Q^{2}=(1-x_{k})
where
x_{k}>x_{i},x_{j}
. i.e,
\tau_{E_{0}}=1-\max\{x_{i}\}
.
. The two distributions are thus equal. However, here we are considering jets of size
R_{s}<1/2
and the following (generic) jet algorithm condition for a pair
(i,j)
to be recombined together to form a jet applies

R_{s}>2(1-\cos\theta_{ij})=\frac{(1-x_{k})}{x_{i}x_{j}}\simeq\frac{(1-x_{k})}{x_{j}},\qquad k\neq i,j,

(C.2)

where the last approximation follows from
x_{k}>x_{i}>x_{j}
(we show in Sec.
5.2.2
that in the limit
R_{s}\to 1/2\Leftrightarrow\theta_{ij}\to\pi/2
the latter approximation yields the exact
\tau
distribution).

The phase space of the two–jet/three–jet boundary for the
\tau_{E_{0}}(p_{1},p_{2})<\tau_{E_{0}}
is given in terms of the energy fractions
2
2

2

Similar limits have been derived for the JADE variable
y
in
[
7
]
, which were also used for
\tau
in the same reference.
as

1>x_{1},x_{2}>1-\tau_{E_{0}},\qquad 1+\tau_{E_{0}}>x_{1}+x_{2},\qquad R^{2}>\frac{4(1-x_{2})}{2-x_{1}-x_{3}},

(C.3)

where we have chosen to recombine the gluon,
p_{3}
, with hard leg
p_{1}
. The other choice, i.e, recombining the gluon with hard leg
p_{2}
, produces identical result. Hence we multiply by a factor of two to account for the latter. Likewise, the phase space boundary for the three–jet configuration whereby the third jet, chosen to be the gluon jet, is vetoed to have energy less than a cut–off
E_{0}
is given by

2>x_{1}+x_{2}>2\left(1-\frac{E_{0}}{Q}\right),\qquad\frac{4(1-x_{2})}{2-x_{1}-x_{3}}>R^{2},\qquad\frac{4(1-x_{1})}{2-x_{2}-x_{3}}>R^{2}.

(C.4)

Due to the soft and collinear singularities of the matrix–element (
C.1
),
x_{1,2}\to 1
, we use the following completion relation in order to integrate the
\tau_{E_{0}}
and
E_{0}
differential distributions

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(0)}+\left[\Sigma_{\rm in}^{(1)}+\Sigma_{\rm out}^{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\int_{0}^{\tau_{E_{0}}}\textrm{\,d}\tau_{E_{0}}^{\prime}\frac{1}{\sigma^{(0)}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(1)}}{\textrm{\,d}\tau_{E_{0}}^{\prime}}+\int_{0}^{E_{0}}\textrm{\,d}E_{3}\frac{1}{\sigma^{(0)}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(1)}}{\textrm{\,d}E_{3}},

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{\rm tot}}{\sigma^{(0)}}-\int_{\tau_{E_{0}}}^{\tau_{E_{0}}^{\max}}\textrm{\,d}\tau_{E_{0}}^{\prime}\frac{1}{\sigma^{(0)}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(1)}}{\textrm{\,d}\tau_{E_{0}}^{\prime}}-\int_{E_{0}}^{Q/2}\textrm{\,d}E_{3}\frac{1}{\sigma^{(0)}}\frac{\textrm{\,d}\sigma^{(1)}}{\textrm{\,d}E_{3}},

where “in” and “out” refer to the regions (
C.3
) and (
C.4
), respectively, and the total cross–section
\sigma^{\rm tot}
, which includes both real (two–jet and three–jet contributions) and virtual corrections, is related to the Born cross–section by
[
7
]

\frac{\sigma^{\rm tot}}{\sigma^{(0)}}=1+\frac{3\mathrm{C_{F}}}{2}\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2}).

(C.6)

Thus inverting the conditions in Eqs. (
C.3
) and (
C.4
), carrying out the integrals in Eq. (
) separately and then adding up with the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})
of Eq. (
C.6
) one obtains Eq. (
5.8
).

C.3
Jet-thrust distribution in SCET

The resummation of the jet-thrust in SCET is presented in the current
section for comparison with pQCD. We shall only present the final form of the
resummed result taken from
Refs.
[
50
,
223
,
46
]
. For a full derivation
and more in depth discussion one should consult the latter references. The only
task we have performed here is the expansion of the full resummed distribution
to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
.

C.3.1
Resummation

The general formula of the resummed distribution for the jet-thrust is
given by
[
50
,
46
]

\frac{\textrm{\,d}\Sigma^{\rm SCET}(\tau_{E_{0}},R)}{\textrm{\,d}\tau_{E_{0}}}=\exp\Big[4S(\mu_{h},\mu_{j})+4S(\mu_{s},\mu_{j})-4A_{H}(\mu_{h},\mu_{s})+4A_{J}(\mu_{j},\mu_{s})\Big]\\
\times\left(\frac{R_{s}}{1-R_{s}}\right)^{-2A_{\Gamma}(\mu_{\omega},\mu_{s})}\left(\frac{Q^{2}}{\mu_{h}^{2}}\right)^{-2A_{\Gamma}(\mu_{h},\mu_{j})}H(Q^{2},\mu_{h})\\
\times S^{\mathrm{out}}_{R}(\omega,\mu_{\omega})\left[\tilde{j}\left(\ln\frac{\mu_{s}Q}{\mu_{j}^{2}}+\partial_{\eta},\mu_{j}\right)\right]^{2}\tilde{s}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\tau_{E_{0}}}(\partial_{\eta},\mu_{s})\\
\times\frac{1}{\tau_{E_{0}}}\left(\frac{\tau_{E_{0}}Q}{\mu_{s}}\right)^{\eta}\frac{e^{-\gamma_{E}\eta}}{\Gamma(\eta)}.

(C.15)

See
[
50
,
46
]
for full notation. In order to compute
the fixed–order expansion of (
C.15
) up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
,
all scales should be set equal (
\mu_{h}=\mu_{j}=\mu_{s}=Q
). In this
limit, the evolution factors
S,A_{J}
and
A_{H}
vanish. The differentiation
w.r.t.
\eta
is carried out using the explicit form of
\tilde{j}
and

\tilde{s}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\tau_{E_{0}}}
. The final result of the integrated distribution may
be cast in the generic form (
5.80
) with the constants and
coefficients of the logs given by

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{C_{F}}\left(-1+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right),

(C.16)

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{C_{F}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{3\pi^{2}}{8}+\frac{\pi^{4}}{72}-6\zeta(3)\right)+\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{C_{A}}\left(\frac{493}{324}+\frac{85\pi^{2}}{24}-\frac{73\pi^{4}}{360}+\frac{283\zeta(3)}{18}\right)+

(C.17)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathrm{C_{F}}\mathrm{T_{F}}n_{f}\left(\frac{7}{81}-\frac{7\pi^{2}}{6}-\frac{22\zeta(3)}{9}\right)+C_{2}^{\mathrm{in}}+C_{2}^{\mathrm{out}},

and

\displaystyle G_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\,\mathrm{C_{F}},

\displaystyle G_{11}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{C_{F}}\left(3-4L_{R_{s}}\right),

\displaystyle G_{10}

\displaystyle G_{23}

\displaystyle=

(C.18)

Appendix D
Full
R
-dependence of clustering coefficients

Here we present a calculation of the dependence of the coefficient
of the clustering logs
\mathcal{F}_{2}
away from the small-angles (thus
small-
R
) approximation. To do so, it is easier to work with
transverse momentum, (pseudo-)rapidity
1
1

1

Recall that
\eta=-\ln\tan(\theta/2)
and
k_{t}=\omega\sin\theta
.
and azimuthal
angle with respect to the
beam
axis,
(k_{t},\eta,\phi)
,
variables instead of energy and polar angles, as performed in sec.

6.3
. We also specialise to the threshold limit in
which the trigged jet is created at
90^{\circ}
to the beam (which is
along the
z
-axis). Our calculations can straightforwardly be
extended to the case where the triggered jet is at an arbitrary
rapidity. We parametrise the outgoing
four-momenta as:

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q}{2}(1,1,0,0),

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Q}{2}(1,-1,0,0),

\displaystyle k_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{ti}(\cosh\eta_{i},\cos\phi_{i},\sin\phi_{i},\sinh\eta_{i}),

(D.1)

with
i=1,2
for the two gluons respectively. In terms of the new
variables, the clustering function (Eq. (
6.3
)) reads:

\Xi_{2}(k_{1},k_{2})=\Theta(d_{1j}-R^{2})\Theta(R^{2}-d_{2j})\Theta(d_{2j}-d_{12}),

(D.2)

with

d_{1j}=\eta_{1}^{2}+\phi_{1}^{2},\qquad d_{2j}=\eta_{2}^{2}+\phi_{2}^{2},\qquad d_{12}=(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})^{2}+(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})^{2}.

(D.3)

In this coordinate system the jet mass becomes:

\rho=\frac{2p_{1}.k_{i}}{Q^{2}/4}=\frac{4k_{ti}}{Q}(\cosh\eta_{i}-\cos\phi_{i})=2x_{i}\left(1-\frac{\cos\phi_{i}}{\cosh\eta_{i}}\right),

(D.4)

when particle
k_{i}
is recombined with the triggered
p_{1}
jet. In
the above we expressed the jet mass in terms of the energy fraction

x_{i}=2\omega_{i}/Q
, with
\omega_{i}=k_{ti}\cosh\eta_{i}
.

The probability of a single virtual soft gluon correction is given
by:

d\Gamma_{i}=-\frac{\textrm{\,d}^{3}\vec{k}_{i}}{2\omega_{i}(2\pi)^{3}}g_{s}^{2}\mathrm{C_{F}}\frac{2(p_{1}.p_{2})}{(p_{1}.k_{i})(p_{2}.k_{i})}=-\frac{\mathrm{C_{F}}\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\frac{\textrm{\,d}x_{i}}{x_{i}}d\eta\frac{\textrm{\,d}\phi_{i}}{\pi}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}\eta_{i}-\cos^{2}\phi_{i}},

(D.5)

where we note here that the collinear limit to the triggered jet

p_{1}
corresponds to
\eta_{i}\to 0

and

\phi_{i}\to 0
.

Thus we can write the correction term due to clustering (Eq.
(
6.4
)) as:

\Sigma^{\rm{clus}}_{2}=\frac{1}{2!}\int\prod_{i}^{2}\textrm{\,d}\Gamma_{i}\Theta\left(x_{i}-\frac{\cosh\eta_{i}}{2(\cosh\eta_{i}-\cos\phi_{i})}\rho\right)\Xi_{2}(k_{1},k_{2}).

(D.6)

Performing the energy-fraction integration yields an expression
identical to Eq. (
6.8
) with the full
R
-dependent
coefficient,
\mathcal{F}_{2}(R)
, given by:

\mathcal{F}_{2}(R)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int\textrm{\,d}\eta_{1}\textrm{\,d}\phi_{1}\textrm{\,d}\eta_{2}\textrm{\,d}\phi_{2}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}\eta_{1}-\cos^{2}\phi_{1}}\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}\eta_{2}-\cos^{2}\phi_{2}}\Xi_{2}(k_{1},k_{2}).

(D.7)

Figure D.1:
Full
R
-dependence of the two-gluon clustering
coefficient
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.

This integration can be performed numerically to extract the value
of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
for arbitrary
R
. However it proves useful, in the
simple case of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
in order to obtain an analytic expression
at least as a power-series, to introduce the polar variables
r
and

\alpha
defined by:

\eta=r\cos\alpha,\qquad\phi=r\sin\alpha.

(D.8)

We rewrite Eq. (
D.7
) as:

\mathcal{F}_{2}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{R}^{\infty}r_{1}\textrm{\,d}r_{1}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\textrm{\,d}\alpha_{1}\int_{0}^{R}r_{2}\textrm{\,d}r_{2}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\textrm{\,d}\alpha_{2}\Theta(2r_{2}\cos(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1})-r_{1})\times\\
\times\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(r_{1}\cos\alpha_{1})-\cos^{2}(r_{1}\sin\alpha_{1})}\,\,\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(r_{2}\cos\alpha_{2})-\cos^{2}(r_{2}\sin\alpha_{2})}.

(D.9)

We note that one can expand the second line of Eq. (
D.9
) in powers of
r_{i}
so as to write the result as a
power-series in
R
. To first order, the second line expands as

1/r_{1}^{2}1/r_{2}^{2}
. We thus immediately identify this integral as the
one for the small-angles approximation, whose result is
\pi^{2}/54\approx 0.183
. Performing higher-order integrals of the expansion
of the integrand yields the following result:

\mathcal{F}_{2}(R)=0.183+0.0246R^{4}+0.00183R^{8}+0.000135R^{12}+\mathcal{O}(R^{16}).

(D.10)

This expansion is actually valid for values of
R
up to order
unity, a claim which is backed-up by fully performing the integral
(
D.9
) numerically via Monte Carlo methods and comparing
to the analytical estimate (
D.10
), Fig.

D.1
. We note that the clustering coefficient varies
very slowly with
R
.
